12
Qudit codes
Much of quantum information science has been built upon the qubit. However, this two-level system,
while powerful, represents only the simplest instance of a unit of quantum information. A more
general and potentially more potent framework emerges from the study of qudits, which are the
higher-dimensional generalizations of qubits and allow us to work with multi-level systems. In this
chapter, we give an overview of qudit error-correcting codes.
12.1
Motivation for and basic background on qudits
Before we explore the motivation for qudit codes, we need to understand the motivation for qudits
to begin with; after all, shouldn’t two-level systems (i.e., qubits) be enough? It turns out, though,
that we can gain a lot by considering higher dimensional Hilbert spaces.
A qudit is simply the q-dimensional generalization of the qubit, for q ≥2. This larger state
space allows for the storage of more information per computational unit and reduced circuit depth.
For example, for qubits, a controlled-U gate only allows one to apply a unitary gate U if the control
qubit is in the state |1⟩or do nothing if the control qubit is in the state |0⟩. However, if we were
working with qutrits, where we have q = 3 basis states |0⟩, |1⟩, and |2⟩, then we think of more
complex control operations: For example, do nothing if the control qutrit is in the state |0⟩, apply
the gate U1 if the qutrit is in the state |1⟩, and apply the gate U2 if the qutrit is in the state |2⟩.
However, if we restrict ourselves to qubit systems, such conditional operations require two control
qubits where, for example, we do nothing if the two control qubits are in the state |00⟩, apply
U1 if they are in state |01⟩or |10⟩, and apply U2 if they are in the state |11⟩. Implementing this
qubit conditional unitaries may also require more gates (e.g., to check for the parity or to do binary
arithmetic on the two control qubits).
More formally, a q-dimensional qudit system can be represented by a q-dimensional Hilbert space
Hq. The Hilbert space Hq is spanned by q orthonormal vectors {|0⟩, |1⟩, . . . , |q −1⟩} such that any
single-qudit state can be written as
|ψ⟩= α0 |0⟩+ α1 |1⟩+ . . . + αq−1 |q −1⟩,
(12.1)
where, as usual, αi ∈C and
|α0|2 + |α1|2 + . . . + |αq−1|2 = 1 .
(12.2)
Just as for qubits, if we have n qudits, then the whole state is a vector in the Hilbert space H⊗n
q ,
spanned by qn orthonormal basis vectors.
To perform quantum computation, we need a set of gates, but these are no longer the qubit
gates we have been working with throughout this tutorial. For qudits, though, we still require gates
to be unitary operators, where any unitary quantum gate U can be arbitrarily approximated by a
universal gate set {Uk}k ⊆U(qn), where U(qn) is the set of qn × qn unitary matrices; of course, for
qubits, we had q = 2. Also just like for the case of qubits, there is more than one such universal
gate set and the choice of gate set can be informed by factors like hardware constraints.
To take two specific examples of q-dimensional generalizations of common gates, consider the
qudit generalizations of the Pauli-X and Z gates, which we denote as Xq and Zq, respectively.
These gates operate on basis states |j⟩as
Xq |j⟩= |j + 1
(mod q)⟩,

Finally, we consider what happens when we have a qudit of dimension q that is not a prime
power. The most straightforward way to deal with this is to factor q into its prime factors and
then treat each prime factor separately with its own Pauli group (e.g., P1(2) ⊗P1(3) for q = 6).
Alternatively, we could make use of the Weyl-Heisenberg group. We do not often consider qudits of
such a non-prime-power composite (as opposed to prime) order, so this is all we will say about this
for now.
12.2
Finite field theory
While the mathematical formalism for qubits is technically based on the finite field F2, we do
not often need to worry about the details of fields beyond some basic notions like the addition
and multiplication rules (e.g., 1 + 1 = 0 (mod 2)). However, when we expand the local Hilbert
space, we need to consider more general fields and mathematical abstractions. In this section, we
introduce finite field theory (also called Galois field theory, after its short-lived founder, Évariste
Galois (1811–1832)), as well as some other important notions from algebra that we will find useful.
We assume that the reader is familiar with the group theoretic concepts from Section 4.1; if
not, we encourage the reader to review that section now. Before we get to finite fields, we need to
introduce rings:
Definition 12.3: Ring
A ring R is a set that is closed under two binary operations: (1) addition: x + y ∈R for any
x, y ∈R and (2) multiplication: xy ∈R for any x, y ∈R. Addition and multiplication satisfy
the following properties for all x, y, z ∈R: (1) associativity: (x + y) + z = x + (y + z) and
(xy)z = x(yz), (2) addition commutativity x + y = y + x, and (3) distributivity x(y + z) =
xy+xz. Also, there exists a multiplicative identity element 1 and an additive identity element
0. For each element x ∈R there exists a unique additive inverse y ∈R, i.e. a unique y ∈R
such that x + y = 0.
If the multiplication in ring R is commutative, that is, xy = yx for all x, y ∈R, then we say
that the ring is a commutative ring. An important concept from ring theory is that of ring ideals:
134


## Page 135


Qudit codes
Definition 12.4: Ring ideal
Let R be a ring. A subset I ⊆R is called an ideal if I is an additive subgroup (meaning that
if x, y ∈I, then so too is x + y) and for any element y ∈R and x ∈I, then yx ∈I.
A generated ideal is constructed as follows. Given a subset S ⊆R of a ring R, the ideal generated
by S, which we denote by (S) (though some references also write this as ⟨S⟩), is the set of elements
of the form P
i riai, where the ai ∈S and the ri ∈R. The generator elements {ai} are not unique.
For example, in the ring of integers, we have (4, 10) = (2). An ideal I ⊆R of a ring R is further
said to be maximal if the only other ideal to fully contain I (i.e., contain all of the elements in I)
is R itself. A quotient ring R/I of an ideal I ⊆R is a ring itself whose elements are the cosets of
I in R with induced operations (addition and multiplication). Combining the notions of a quotient
ring and a maximal ideal, it is a fact that R/I is a field if and only if I is a maximal ideal; we will
see this below more explicitly.
With this background on ring theory, we can now define a finite field. We start by defining a
field in general:
Definition 12.5: Field
A field is a commutative ring in which every nonzero element has a multiplicative inverse.
Then, more specifically, we have the simple following definition:
Definition 12.6: Finite field
A finite field is a field with a finite number of elements.
We denote a field with q ∈N elements by Fq, though the notation GF(q) (for Galois f ield is also
common). If q is prime, then Fq is isomorphic to Z/qZ, that is, the integers modulo q. We write
this as Fq ∼= Z/qZ. If q = pm for some prime p and integer m ≥2 (i.e., q is a prime power), then
Fq is not isomorphic to Z/qZ. We call p the characteristic of the field. The number of elements
in a finite field, which we call the order of the field (i.e., q), is always a prime or a prime power;
there are no finite fields of order that is a composite number, such as 6. We can formalize this in
the following theorem:
Theorem 12.1: Existence and uniqueness theorem for finite fields
For every q = pm for prime p and integer m ≥1, there exists a field with q elements.
Furthermore, any two fields with q elements are isomorphic. Thus, the field Fq is unique.
This is a well-known result and the proof is beyond the scope of this tutorial, but we refer the
reader to any good set of lecture notes or textbooks [93] on finite field theory. We can construct a
finite field using polynomials, specifically polynomial quotients. To see this, we need one additional
concept, that of irreducibility:
Definition 12.7: Polynomial irreducibility
A polynomial f(x) ∈Fq[x] is irreducible if it cannot be factored into two polynomials
g(x), h(x) ∈Fq[x] such that the degrees of both g(x) and h(x) are less than the degree
135


## Page 136


Qudit codes
of f(x).
With this in mind, we can construct a finite field Fq with q = pm elements, for prime p and
m ≥1 as follows. Let f(x) ∈Fp[x] be an irreducible polynomial, as defined in Definition 12.7.
Then,
Fq ∼= Fp[x]/(f(x)) .
(12.14)
Note the parentheses around f(x), denoting this as a ring ideal, as defined in Definition 12.4. Thus,
we define higher-order finite fields in terms of their characteristic (i.e., p) field. One of the simplest
examples is the construction of the field F4:
Example 12.1: Finite field with four elements
Let p = 2 and f(x) = x2 + x + 1, which is irreducible over F2. We can construct F4 as
F4 = F2[x]/(x2 + x + 1) ,
(12.15)
where the four elements of F4 are {0, 1, x, x + 1}. Arithmetic is then done modulo x2 + x + 1.
F4 is the smallest non-prime field and is thus important for understanding higher-order finite
fields.
Notably, every function f : Fq →Fq can be represented as a polynomial. We can also consider
finite fields from a vector space perspective, as we hinted at in Section 12.1. That is, the field Fpm
can be worked with as an m-dimensional vector space over Fp. This allows us to use the vast toolbox
of linear algebra where we keep in mind that arithmetic operations are done over Z/pZ ∼= Fp.
To conclude, we highlight some interesting properties of finite fields. First, we note that the
nonzero elements of a finite field Fq, which we denote by F×
q , form a cyclic group under multiplication:
F×
q ∼= Cq−1 .
(12.16)
A cyclic group Cq−1 is simply a group that is generated by a single element under multiplication.
To connect this concept to finite fields, we note that there is some element α ∈F×
q that we call
primitive that gives us the whole of F×
q :
F×
q = {αk : 0 ≤k < q −1} .
(12.17)
We can also denote this as F×
q = ⟨α⟩. Furthermore, there are exactly φ(q −1) primitive elements
in any multiplicative group, where φ(n) is a function called Euler’s totient function, which counts
the number of positive integers up to n that are coprime to n. Finally, we note that a finite field
Fpn has a subfield of size Fpm if and only if m|n, that is, m divides n. Each such subfield is unique.
12.3
Major families of qudit stabilizer codes
The main class of qudit codes that we focus on in this chapter is that of qudit stabilizer codes. There
are two axes along which we can define a qudit stabilizer code: the algebra upon which the code
is built and the construction style. By the “algebra,” we mean the group or field that all of our
subsequent operations are done over, which is, of course, determined by the local dimension of the
qudit. Thus, for a qudit code with local qudit dimension q, we could have codes defined over the
integers modulo q, Zq, or a finite field Fq, depending on what q is, to name two examples. The
“construction” is the other ingredient that gives us a qudit code, and this determines the structure
136


## Page 137


Qudit codes
of the code. Examples include CSS, non-CSS, topological, homological, LDPC, and graph-based
codes.
12.3.1
Algebra
We start by looking at different qudit stabilizer codes based on their algebra, that is, what group
or field the local qudit dimension is defined over.
Modular-qudit stabilizer codes.
We start by considering modular-qudit stabilizer codes
.
These codes are the most straightforward generalization of qubit stabilizer codes and were introduced
concurrently with the qubit formalism in Daniel Gottesman’s PhD thesis [63]. As was the case for
qubits, the fundamental group that we work with to build up our stabilizer codes is the Pauli group,
except we now consider the modular-qudit Pauli group [244], which we define as follows:
Definition 12.8: Modular qudit Pauli group

is generated by all X(a), Z(b), and a global phase τ = e2πi/q that is a qth root of unity. In

Box 12.1: Modules
A module can be thought of as a generalization of a vector space, where the field of scalars is
replaced by a ring. The ring can be either commutative or non-commutative. Modules have
important applications in commutative algebra, homological algebra, algebraic geometry, and
algebraic topology. For example, in commutative algebra, ideals and quotient rings (which
we defined above in Section 12.2) are modules. Much of the work with modules has to do
with extending the properties of vector spaces to well-behaved rings, but there are several
complications that arise when doing this. For example, not all modules have a basis, which
is, of course, untrue for vector spaces.
Let R be a ring (see Definition 12.3). A left R-module is a set M with
1. a binary operation + on M in which M is an abelian group and
2. an action of R on M (i.e., a map R×M →M) denoted by rm for all r ∈R and m ∈M
satisfying:
(a) (r + s)m = rm + sm for all r, s ∈R, m ∈M,
(b) (rs)m = r(sm) for all r, s ∈R, m ∈M, and
(c) r(m + n) = rm + rn for all r ∈R, m, n ∈M.
If R has an identity element 1, then we further have a unital condition, that is,
(d) 1m = m for all m ∈M.
In this work, we assume rings are unital.
Right R-modules are defined analogously and if R is commutative, then we can turn a left
R-module M into a right R-module by taking rm = mr for r ∈R and m ∈M.
A common example of a module is a Z-module, where Z is, of course, the set of integers. Let
R = Z and A be any abelian group with binary operation +. To make A into a Z-module,
we define for any n ∈Z and a ∈A the quantity na, where
na =

where 0 is the additive identity in A. This is the only possible action of Z on A making it
a unital Z-module. Since A was taken to be any abelian group, we can conclude that every
abelian group is a Z-module.

If A is an abelian group with element x of finite order n, then nx = 0. That is, for Z-modules,
there exist nonzero elements x ∈A such that nx = 0 for n ̸= 0, which is not true for vector
spaces. If A has order m, then mx = 0 for all x ∈A and we say that A is a module over
Z/mZ. Furthermore, if p is prime and A is abelian such that px = 0 for all x ∈A, then A
is a Z/pZ-module. However, we know that Z/pZ ∼= Fp, so A is also an Fp-module, which is
just a vector space over Fp.

While for modular-qudit stabilizer codes we allowed q to be any

get Galois-qudit stabilizer codes
[246]. That is, we take q = pm for some prime p and m ≥1.

elements from Fq. Thus, a single-qudit Pauli operator XaZb for a, b ∈Fq is written as (a|b) ∈F2
q.
The notation for n Galois qudits follows analogously. Furthermore, the Pauli operators are defined

n Galois-qudits to encode k logical qudits with codespace dimension K = qk and distance d using
the notation Jn, k, dKq. Note, though, the subscript q rather than Zq to denote that these codes are
defined over a finite field rather than a ring. Furthermore, just as was the case for modular-qudit
stabilizer codes, the logical codespace dimension does not need to be an integer, as we can write
K = qn−r/m, where r is the number of generators of the stabilizer group, and the quantity r/m is
not necessarily an integer. The distance means the same thing as it did for modular-qudit stabilizer
codes as well, namely, the minimum weight of a Galois-qudit Pauli string yielding a nontrivial logical
operator. Thus, the code Jn, k, dKq detects and corrects erasure errors on d −1 qudits and corrects
errors on at most ⌊d−1
2 ⌋qudits.
True Galois-qudit stabilizer codes.
Finally, we can consider a subclass of Galois-qudit sta-
bilizer codes whose stabilizers’ symplectic representation forms a linear subspace. Such codes are
called true Galois-qudit stabilizer codes
. Here, the vectors comprising the stabilizer group form a
subspace in which addition and multiplication are closed by elements of Fq. Note that this definition
adds multiplication, where for the Galois-qudit stabilizer codes in the previous section, we only had
closure under addition. The number of generators r is thus an integer multiple of m, so the quotient
r/m is an integer. Thus, the code has a logical codespace dimension K = qn−r/m, which is now
always an integer, which is nice. Everything else about these codes (e.g., the parity-check matrices,
the code parameters, etc.) is the same as for the Galois-qudit stabilizer codes.

12.3.2
Construction style
While the algebraic structure told us how to represent the stabilizers and the commutation re-
lations between Pauli operators, the code construction style tells us how to build the commuting
check operators. We consider four construction styles: CSS, non-CSS, topological/homological, and
LDPC.
CSS-type.
Consider the true Galois-qudit stabilizer codes from above. If we have such a code
and we have a set of stabilizer generators that are either X- or Z-type Pauli strings, then we
have a Galois-qudit CSS code. As such, these codes can be defined using chain complexes over Fq
by extending the techniques that map CSS codes to homology groups for qubits to qudits. The
stabilizer generator matrix is now nicely given as

Of course, the nice thing about CSS qudit codes is that they can be derived from q-ary linear codes,
where we take one code to be an [n, kX, dX]q code CX and the other to be an [n, kZ, dZ]q code CZ
such that C⊥

Furthermore, we associate HX with the parity-check matrix of the classical code CX and HZ as the
parity-check matrix of the classical code CZ.
Non-CSS-type.
For non-CSS-type qudit codes, we do not have the nice separation between X-
and Z-type stabilizers. These codes generally refer to self-orthogonal codes under a given symplectic
form and they relate to the self-orthogonal additive codes over Fq2 that we mentioned above. This
allows for the development of quantum Hamming codes, quantum BCH codes, and other quantum
analogues of popular classical q-ary codes over Fq.
Topological and homological.
As we alluded to above in our discussion of CSS-type qudit codes,
we can define qudit stabilizer codes from chain complexes and take advantage of the tools from
homological algebra, which we considered earlier in this tutorial (see, for example, Section 5.1.2).
Basic examples of such codes include qudit versions of the surface code and the toric code. For
example, for qudit surface codes, stabilizer generators are of X- and Z-type and they act on only a
few qudits. We associate these with the stars and plaquettes of a tessellation of a two-dimensional
surface. Other examples include the qudit color codes [247], which are just qudit generalizations of
the qubit color codes.
LDPC.
Finally, there exist qudit generalizations of several major classes of LDPC codes, where the
stabilizers have the benefit of being sparse. Examples of such qudit LDPC codes [239, 248] include
qudit generalizations of the currently-popular bivariate bicycle codes, qudit hypergraph product
codes (also very popular), subsystem hypergraph (SHYPS) codes, high-dimensional expander codes,
and fiber bundle codes. Qudit LDPC codes inherit many of the favorable qualities of qubit LDPC
codes, such as reduced physical qudit overhead, high encoding rates, and sparsity in the check
operators, requiring fewer check measurements.

12.5
Experimental realizations of qudit codes
There have been several examples of experimental realizations of qudit-based systems using vari-
ous platforms, such as superconducting [250–255], photonic [256–259], trapped-ion [260–263], and
circuit quantum electrodynamics (QED) [264] devices. However, these are just experimentally im-
plementing qudit systems; there has been less, but still notable, work on implementing qudit-based
141


## Page 142


Qudit codes
error correction on real devices. We highlight two examples in this section. The first [265] is an
experimental demonstration of a qutrit toric code, which demonstrated the preparation and mea-
surement of a Z3 toric code ground state on 24 qutrits. The second [264], which received quite a bit
of attention in the community and the press, demonstrated encoding a logical qutrit and a logical
ququart using a single-mode square-lattice GKP qudit. While we did not discuss GKP codes in the
context of qudits, we refer the reader to Section 8 for more on bosonic codes. Both of these early
demonstrations show that implementing error-corrected qudit systems is experimentally viable and
pave the way to future, larger-scale experiments.

Fault tolerance I
A quantum error-correcting code protects states.
In contrast, a fault-tolerant protocol protects
processes [24, 64, 69, 89].
The core difficulty is not only detecting and correcting errors, but
ensuring that the mechanism of correction does not create more errors than it removes. A location
is an elementary circuit component: a one- or two-qubit gate, state preparation, measurement, or
an idle step. A fault is a location at which the physical operation deviates from the ideal one.
An ideal circuit contains abstract logical locations: perfect preparations, perfect gates, perfect
waits, and perfect measurements. A fault-tolerant implementation replaces each logical location
by a gadget operating on encoded data. Examples include an encoded CNOT gadget, an encoded
measurement gadget, and an error-correction gadget.
In this chapter, we make the first steps
in understanding fault-tolerant quantum computation (FTQC), the pinnacle of the quantum error
correction enterprise.
13.1
Error propagation
Suppose we are given a circuit with faulty components. If each location of this circuit fails with
probability at most p, then the failure probability associated with a fixed output location scales
linearly as O(p). For a computation with N gates, a union bound gives an overall failure probability
scaling as O(Np). Error correction becomes useful only when encoded gadgets suppress the effective
logical error rate below the underlying physical error rate.
Merely performing error correction
periodically is not sufficient to prevent the buildup of errors, even if it is applied after every encoded
gate. Two reasons for this include:
1. Error propagation through gates: Encoded gates can cause errors to propagate.
For
example, let U be the CNOT gate. The propagation of a Pauli-X error on the control qubit
is given by:
U(X ⊗I)U† = X ⊗X
(13.1)
Similarly, a Z error on the target qubit propagates backwards:
U(I ⊗Z)U† = Z ⊗Z
(13.2)
A single fault in a two-qubit gate can thus introduce two errors in the code block, as seen
in Figure 26.
2. Errors introduced by correction: Error correction itself can introduce errors on the en-
coded qubits if the ancilla qubits or the gates used to measure the syndrome are faulty.
With this in mind, we define fault tolerance as follows:

Fault tolerance I
Definition 13.1: Fault tolerance
For a code correcting up to t errors per block, a procedure is fault-tolerant if any pattern
consisting of at most s ≤t faults during the procedure, together with at most r ≤t −s
pre-existing errors on each input block, produces at most a correctable number of errors on
each output block, that is, at most t errors per output block, and does not alter the encoded
logical action.
To satisfy the fault tolerance condition, we design encoded operations such that a failure any-
where during the implementation can only propagate to a small number of qubits on each block.
The most robust way to achieve this is via transversality.
Definition 13.2: Transversal gate
Let a code block consist of n physical qubits. An encoded gate acting on one or more blocks
is transversal if it is implemented as a tensor product of gates that couple only corresponding
qubits across blocks and never couple two qubits within the same block. For a one-block
gate,
Utrans =

O
i=1
Ui.
(13.3)
For a two-block transversal gate, the ith qubit of one block may interact only with the ith
qubit of the other block.

Syndrome extraction schemes
Consider an n-qubit stabilizer code with stabilizer group S ⊂Pn and generators
S = ⟨S1, . . . , Sr⟩,
Sj ∈{I, X, Y, Z}⊗n .

S = ⟨S(X)

a

b
contains only Z and I. The goal of syndrome
extraction is to measure the eigenvalues sj ∈{±1} of the generators Sj fault-tolerantly, that is, such
that a single fault does not propagate to multiple data qubits. We will discuss three different fault-
tolerant schemes for syndrome extraction, in the sense formalized later in Definition 13.3.
13.2.1
Shor scheme
Peter Shor introduced [64] the first method for fault-tolerant syndrome extraction. Shor’s method
extracts syndromes stabilizer-by-stabilizer, utilizing cat states or GHZ states. For a weight-w sta-
bilizer, Shor’s scheme prepares an ancilla in the w-qubit state:
|catw⟩=
1
√
2
 |0⟩⊗w + |1⟩⊗w
.
(13.12)
The protocol proceeds in three steps:
1. Preparation and verification: The cat state is prepared using a cascade of CNOT gates.
However, a single fault during this preparation circuit could easily create a weight-2 error
in the cat state.
To prevent this from corrupting the data qubits, the cat state must be
rigorously verified.
Note that a cat state is a repetition code, one uses additional ancilla
qubits to measure the parity of adjacent qubits in the cat state (checking that it is indeed
in the repetition space). If any verification check fails, the corrupted cat state is instantly
discarded, and a new one is prepared.
2. Transversal CNOT gates: Once accepted by the verification checks, the cat state interacts
with the data block via transversal CNOT gates. Crucially, the ith qubit of the cat state
interacts exclusively with the ith data qubit involved in the stabilizer. Because the interaction
is transversal, an error on the cat state can only ever propagate to a single data qubit.
3. Measurement and majority voting: After the coupling, the ancilla is measured and
the parity of its outcomes yields the stabilizer eigenvalue. Because ancilla preparation and
measurement are noisy, a single extraction of the same stabilizer is not usually trusted. Instead,
the measurement is repeated an odd number of times and a majority vote is taken. The data
are corrected only after a consistent syndrome is obtained or after a decoding rule is applied
to the repeated outcomes.
Shor extraction is conceptually clean and applies to general stabilizer codes, not only CSS codes.
Its main cost is ancilla overhead and time: a separate cat state is needed for each stabilizer mea-
surement, and repeated measurements are typically required.
145

Fault tolerance I
13.2.2
Steane scheme
Steane extraction is tailored to CSS codes and exploits the fact that the X-type and Z-type stabi-
lizers arise from classical linear parity checks [58]. Instead of measuring each stabilizer individually,
one uses an entire encoded ancilla block. To extract the syndrome of Z-type stabilizers (equiva-
lently, to diagnose X errors on the data), prepare an ancilla in the encoded state
0

. Apply a
transversal CNOT from the data block to the ancilla block. Then measure the ancilla transversally
in the computational basis and classically decode the outcome using the parity-check matrix of the
underlying classical code. The measured bit string contains the same syndrome information that
would be produced by measuring the Z-type stabilizers directly.
Similarly, to extract the syndrome of X-type stabilizers (equivalently, to diagnose Z errors),
prepare |+⟩= H
0

, apply a transversal CNOT in the opposite orientation, measure the ancilla
transversally in the X basis, and classically decode the resulting outcomes. The fault tolerance
mechanism is twofold:
1. The data-ancilla interaction is transversal, so one gate fault creates at most one error in each
block.
2. The ancilla is itself an encoded state, so the syndrome is extracted in a highly redundant form
that can be decoded classically.
The subtle point is ancilla preparation. A fault during the preparation of
0

or |+⟩may create a
correlated, high-weight ancilla error. If the ancilla is used without further checks, that correlated
error can propagate through the transversal CNOT gates to several data qubits. Therefore, the
ancilla must either be verified before use or decoded afterward so that dangerous correlated faults
can be identified and compensated in the Pauli frame. Both approaches appear in the literature;
ancilla decoding is especially important when post-selection is expensive.
Compared with Shor
extraction, Steane extraction uses larger ancillas but fewer rounds of interaction and measurement.
On CSS codes such as the Steane code itself, it is usually far more efficient.
13.2.3
Knill scheme
Knill’s method performs error correction by teleporting the logical state through a verified encoded
Bell pair [266]. Prepare two encoded blocks in the logical Bell state
Φ+
E
=
1

 00

11

.
(13.13)
One half of this Bell pair is then Bell-measured against the incoming data block, using only transver-
sal gates and transversal measurements. As in ordinary quantum teleportation, the unknown logical
state is transferred to the second half of the Bell pair, up to a Pauli correction determined by the
Bell-measurement outcomes. The key observation is that the Bell measurement simultaneously re-
veals the error syndrome. In effect, teleportation and error correction are fused into a single step.
Any correction can be tracked in software as an update of the Pauli frame, so physical recovery
operations need not be applied immediately. The Knill scheme has two important practical features:
1. Ancilla verification can be done aggressively offline. Bad Bell pairs are discarded before they
ever interact with the data.
2. Because the data block is consumed by a teleportation measurement, many faults are converted
into known Pauli-frame updates rather than lingering as unknown physical errors.
This often leads to excellent threshold estimates in architectures that can afford abundant ancilla
factories. The tradeoff is higher preparation overhead and more demanding ancilla logistics.

13.3
Formalism for fault tolerance
To make the threshold theorem precise, one must state exactly what a fault-tolerant gadget is
required to do [70, 89]. Let C be an Jn, k, 2t+1K code, so that up to t physical errors are correctable
on each block.
13.3.1
Error sets and filters
For a subset R ⊆{1, . . . , n} of the n qubit indices, let E(R) denote the linear span of Pauli operators
supported on R. The subspace of states with at most r errors relative to the codespace is
Vr =
X
|R|≤r
E(R)C.
(13.14)
An ideal r-filter is the projector onto Vr. Informally, an n-qubit block state passes an r-filter exactly
when it differs from some codeword by errors on at most r qubits. This language is convenient
because fault tolerance is not merely a statement about the final decoded state, it is also a statement
about the intermediate encoded state remaining inside the correctable sector.
Definition 13.3: Fault-tolerant error-correction gadget
An error-correction gadget for an Jn, k, 2t + 1K code is t-fault-tolerant if for every r, s ≥0
with r + s ≤t the following hold:
1. Recovery property. If the gadget contains at most s faults, then its output passes
an s-filter.
2. Correctness property. If the input passes an r-filter and the gadget contains at most
s faults, then ideal decoding of the output gives the same state as ideal decoding of the
input.
147


## Page 148


Fault tolerance I
The first property says that faults occurring during error correction leave behind at most a
correctable number of residual errors. The second property says that if the total number of pre-
existing input errors and faults inside the gadget is at most t, then the output is correctly decodable
to the same logical state. For distance-3 codes (t = 1), the definition reduces to two memorable
conditions:
1. If one fault occurs during error correction, the output block has at most one error;
2. If the input block has at most one error and the gadget is otherwise fault-free, the output is
logically correct.
13.3.2
Fault-tolerant gate gadgets
A gate gadget must also satisfy two requirements. First, it must control how pre-existing errors and
internal faults propagate to the outputs. Second, after decoding, it must implement the intended
logical gate.
Definition 13.4: Fault-tolerant gate gadget
Consider a logical gate acting on one or more encoded blocks of an Jn, k, 2t+1K code. A gate
gadget is t-fault-tolerant if for every r, s ≥0 with r + s ≤t:
1. Error-propagation property. If each input block passes an r-filter and the gadget
contains at most s faults, then each output block passes an (r + s)-filter.
2. Logical correctness. If each input block passes an r-filter and the gadget contains
at most s faults, then ideal decoding of the output is the same as applying the ideal
logical gate to the ideally decoded input. Equivalently, if D denotes ideal decoding and
GU
s denotes any implementation of the logical gate U with at most s faults, then logical
correctness requires
D
 GU
s (ρ)

= UD(ρ)U†
(13.15)
for every admissible input state ρ satisfying the filter condition above.
For transversal gadgets, the propagation property is usually immediate: a single physical fault
affects only the corresponding output qubits, so the number of errors per block grows additively
rather than catastrophically.
13.3.3
Rectangles and extended rectangles
Threshold proofs are organized around the replacement of each ideal logical location by a level-
1 gadget. The gadget implementing a logical gate together with the error-correction gadgets that
follow it on every output block is called a rectangle (Rec). If one includes the leading error-correction
gadgets on the input blocks as well, one obtains an extended rectangle (exRec).
The exRec is the natural unit of analysis because faults in the leading error correction can
combine with faults inside the gate gadget.
A distance-3 exRec is called good if it contains at
most one fault. The usual exRec-correctness lemma states that every good exRec simulates the
ideal logical location correctly. This lemma is the bridge from local gadget properties to the global
threshold theorem.
148


## Page 149


Fault tolerance I
13.3.4
Strong and weak fault-tolerant error correction
In the literature, one encounters both strong and weak notions of fault-tolerant error correction [89,
267]:
• Strong fault-tolerant EC requires the recovery property unconditionally: if the EC gadget
contains at most s faults, then regardless of the detailed input state the output lies within
the s-error sector of the code. This notion is highly composable and is especially convenient
in recursive proofs.
• Weak fault-tolerant EC imposes correctness only for the class of inputs that can arise during
a fault-tolerant computation, for example inputs already known to pass an r-filter for suitable
r. In other words, the gadget is guaranteed to work on admissible states even if it need not
behave well on arbitrary adversarial inputs.
Weak fault tolerance is often sufficient in practice because encoded gadgets are never fed completely
arbitrary states; they are fed outputs of previous gadgets that already satisfy filter conditions.
Strong fault tolerance is conceptually cleaner and easier to compose, but it can be more expensive
to realize. The distinction matters when optimizing ancilla verification, post-selection, and decoder
scheduling.
13.4
Noise models
Threshold theorems do not apply to arbitrary noise without assumptions.
The meaning of the
physical error rate depends on the class of allowed correlations in space and time. Three broad
models appear repeatedly.
13.4.1
Stochastic noise
The simplest model assigns a fault probability to each location. In an independent stochastic model,
each location fails independently with probability p. More general and more useful is local stochastic
noise: for every set R of locations [70],
Pr[all locations in R are faulty] ≤p|R| .
(13.16)
This bound allows arbitrary correlations among faults, provided large correlated sets are exponen-
tially suppressed. The local stochastic model is particularly convenient for combinatorial threshold
proofs because the probability of a malignant set of m faulty locations is at most pm. A common
special case is stochastic Pauli noise, in which each faulty location is followed by a Pauli channel,
N(ρ) =
X
P∈Pn
qP PρP ,
X
P
qP = 1 .
(13.17)
Because stabilizer propagation is easy to analyze under Pauli channels, this model is ubiquitous in
simulations and decoder benchmarking.
13.4.2
Non-Markovian noise
A Markovian noise model describes each time step by a completely positive trace-preserving map
that depends only on the current system state. In contrast, non-Markovian noise allows the envi-
ronment to retain memory. A standard Hamiltonian model writes
H(t) = HS(t) + HB(t) + HSB(t) ,
(13.18)

where HSB(t) couples the computer to its environment. Errors need not be stochastic events with
well-defined probabilities; they may interfere coherently across time.
Threshold theorems nevertheless survive under suitable locality assumptions [69].
Roughly
speaking, if the system-bath coupling is local and sufficiently weak in operator norm, then the
sum of all bad fault paths remains bounded and concatenation still suppresses the effective logical
noise. The proof is more delicate because amplitudes, not probabilities, are combined, and coherent
cancellation or reinforcement must be controlled.
13.4.3
Adversarial noise
The strongest model allows the faulty operation at each bad location to be chosen adversarially,
possibly depending on the entire fault pattern, subject only to a locality or norm bound. This worst-
case viewpoint is often formulated in terms of the diamond norm distance between the implemented
and ideal operations.
Adversarial noise is mathematically severe: one no longer assumes that errors are random Pauli
flips or even stochastic events. The strength of fault tolerance in this regime comes from geometry
and locality rather than from any favorable probabilistic structure. Rigorous thresholds exist for
several adversarial or coherent models, but the proven threshold constants are generally smaller
than the thresholds observed under phenomenological stochastic noise.
13.5
Threshold theorem
The discovery of QEC was a monumental step, but it introduced a seemingly fatal paradox: the
very circuits used to perform error correction (encoding, syndrome measurement, and recovery) are
themselves composed of noisy physical gates. This raises a critical question: does the process of
error correction introduce more errors than it fixes? The answer, which provides the theoretical
foundation for all of scalable quantum computing, is given by the threshold theorem. Informally, it
states:
If the error rate p of each physical component in a quantum computer (qubit preparation,
gates, measurements, memory) is below a certain constant value, the accuracy threshold
pth, then it is possible to perform an arbitrarily long quantum computation with an
arbitrarily low logical error rate ϵ.
150


## Page 151


Fault tolerance I
The overhead required to achieve this fault tolerance, in terms of the number of physical qubits
and gates, scales efficiently, typically as a polynomial in the logarithm of the circuit size and 1/ϵ. The
theorem is best understood through a quantitative recursion. Let p(ℓ)

of a level-ℓexRec, and let A be an upper bound on the number of malignant sets of size t + 1 inside
the largest level-1 exRec. Then for sufficiently small p,

Why the overhead is efficient

It depends on the code, the gadget construction,
the decoder, the connectivity constraints, and the noise model. Rigorous threshold values derived
from malignant-set counting are typically more conservative than thresholds estimated numerically
for specific architectures. Concatenation is the recursive architecture that turns the local gadget
properties of the previous sections into asymptotically reliable computation [24, 70, 89].
13.6
Recursive encoding
Let C be an Jn, k, dK code. In one level of encoding, each logical block is mapped to n physical
qubits. In two levels of concatenation, each of those n qubits is itself re-encoded using the same
code. Continuing recursively, a level-ℓencoded block uses nℓphysical qubits. For self-concatenation
of an Jn, 1, dK code, the effective parameters are Jnℓ, 1, dℓK. The multiplicative distance law follows
because an error must cause a logical fault at the outer level and, within each affected outer-level
qubit, must also cause a logical fault at the next inner level, and so on recursively. As an example,
concatenating the Steane J7, 1, 3K code twice yields a J49, 1, 9K code, and concatenating by ℓlevels
gives a J7ℓ, 1, 3ℓK code.
13.7
Recursive gadgets
Concatenation is not merely recursive state encoding. Every logical location in the ideal circuit
is replaced by a level-1 gadget. Then every physical location inside that gadget is replaced by its
own level-1 gadget to create a level-2 gadget, and so on. Thus a level-ℓencoded gate is a gadget
built from level-(ℓ−1) encoded gates, measurements, preparations, and error-correction steps. This
hierarchical construction ensures self-similarity: once one proves that good level-1 exRecs are correct,
the same argument applies at every higher level because a level-ℓexRec behaves as a single effective
location for the level-(ℓ+ 1) simulation.
13.8
Why concatenation works
Suppose a code corrects one error and the level-1 gadgets are designed so that any single fault inside
an exRec is harmless. Then a logical failure at level 1 requires at least two faults in a malignant
arrangement. At level 2, a logical failure requires at least two level-1 exRec failures, each of which
already requires two physical faults, so the dominant contribution is fourth order. Repeating this
recursively yields the double-exponential suppression in Eq. (13.22). This recursive suppression is
the conceptual heart of concatenated fault tolerance: the effective error model becomes progressively
more benign at higher levels of encoding, provided the physical noise starts below threshold.
152


## Page 153


Fault tolerance I
13.9
Why transversality is powerful
Suppose a code corrects one error per block. If a single X error enters a transversal CNOT on the
control block, the error may spread to the corresponding qubit in the target block, but each block
still contains only one error. The gate is therefore compatible with distance-3 correction. This is
exactly the right notion of safe error spread: faults may duplicate across blocks, but they may not
proliferate within a block. Many CSS codes admit useful transversal gates. For example:
• transversal CNOT is logical CNOT for any CSS code built from nested classical codes;
• self-dual CSS codes often admit transversal Hadamard;
• doubly-even CSS codes can admit a transversal phase gate, sometimes up to adjoint conven-
tion, as in the Steane code.
Despite their usefulness, transversal gates alone can never provide a universal gate set on a finite-
dimensional quantum code. In fact, the Eastin-Knill theorem says that no quantum error-correcting
code that detects arbitrary single-subsystem errors can realize a universal set of logical gates using
only transversal unitary gates [159]. It does not say that transversal gates are rare or unhelpful;
rather, it says that any finite code with nontrivial error-detecting power must sacrifice universality
if one insists on transversality alone. In practice, this means that a fault-tolerant architecture needs
an additional ingredient beyond transversal Clifford operations. Common remedies include magic-
state distillation and injection, code switching, gauge fixing, lattice surgery, and pieceable fault
tolerance.
Transversality is a special case of a broader geometric concept: locality-preserving logical gates.
These are logical operations that map local errors to local errors and can therefore be implemented
without long-range spread of damage.
For topological stabilizer codes, such gates are severely
constrained. The Bravyi-König theorem says that for a D-dimensional topological stabilizer code,
every locality-preserving logical gate belongs to the Dth level of the Clifford hierarchy [268]. The
consequence is especially striking in two dimensions. In a two-dimensional topological stabilizer
code, every locality-preserving logical gate is Clifford. Therefore, no two-dimensional topological
stabilizer code can realize a universal fault-tolerant gate set by transversal or any other locality-
preserving mechanism alone. In three dimensions one may obtain logical gates from the third level
of the Clifford hierarchy, such as CCZ in suitable codes, but even that is still not universal by itself.
Transversal gates are indispensable because they are simple, local, and naturally fault-tolerant.
But the Eastin-Knill and Bravyi-König theorems show that fault-tolerant universality cannot come
from transversality alone. Modern architectures therefore combine a “cheap” fault-tolerant gate set,
usually Clifford operations, with a more expensive resource-generation procedure that supplies the
missing non-Clifford ingredients.

14
Fault tolerance II
In this second part on fault tolerance, we will cover three topics: (1) the seminal work on a constant-
overhead FTQC scheme that uses a family of qLDPC codes in Sections 14.1 to 14.4, (2) a magic
state distillation scheme that distills 10 noisy magic states to two magic states with suppressed
error in Section 14.5, (3) code-switching protocols that allows for an implementation of a universal
transversal gate set by using two codes.
We will first discuss the proof of the existence of a decoding threshold for exponential error
suppression in the code distance for qLDPC codes in the case of ideal syndrome extraction (Sec-
tion 14.2) and noisy syndrome extraction (Section 14.3). Then, in Section 14.4, we will discuss the
overall FTQC scheme and derive a threshold for the entire scheme in detail. Next, in Section 14.5
we will discuss a simple magic state distillation scheme based on a 4-qubit error-detecting code and
show how it distills 10 copies of noisy magic states to two magic states with quadratic suppres-
sion of their noise rate. Lastly, in Section 14.6, we will discuss two code-switching protocols as an
alternative protocol to magic state distillation to enable a universal FTQC.
14.1
Constant-overhead FTQC scheme using qLDPC codes
Here we discuss the FTQC scheme with a family of qLDPC codes with non-vanishing asymptotic
encoding and distance rate proposed in [229].
Namely, we consider a family of qLDPC codes
Q1, Q2, . . . with parameters Jni, ki, diK such that each code has a non-vanishing asymptotic encoding
rate R = limi→∞ki
ni > 0 and a non-constant distance limi→∞di = ∞. We will first discuss two
important results showing that there exists a decoder for such a family of codes such that errors
are suppressed exponentially in the code distance di whenever physical error rates are below some
threshold values.
First, in Section 14.2 we will show a decoding threshold when the syndrome
extraction circuit is assumed to be ideal. Then, in Section 14.3 we will show that such decoding
threshold still exists even when the syndrome extraction circuit is noisy, particularly by doing
multiple cycles of syndrome extraction. Lastly in Section 14.4, we will give a detailed proof on the
existence of a threshold for this constant-overhead FTQC scheme.
Here we will consider a local stochastic noise model where the probability of faults of a certain
size occurring decays exponentially with the fault size.
Definition 14.1: Local stochastic noise
For a quantum circuit C with locations loc(C), a local stochastic noise model with rate p
implies that the probability of faults on all locations in a subset S ⊆loc(C) is given by:
Pr[All locations in S are faulty] ≤p|S| .
(14.1)
For an n-qubit system, a local stochastic noise with rate p implies that the probability of
faults on all qubits in a set S ⊆[n] is given by
Pr[All qubits in S are faulty] ≤p|S| .
(14.2)
14.2
Decoding threshold for ideal syndrome extraction
Theorem 3 of Ref. [229] shows that for a family {Qi}i of (r, c)-qLDPC codes with parameters
Jni, ki, diK with ni →∞, di →∞, local stochastic noise with rate p, there exists a decoding
algorithm and a threshold p0 > 0 such that the logical error rate is pL = O(ni(p/p0)di/2) whenever
154


## Page 155


Fault tolerance II
p < p0.
This result generalizes the result by Kovalev and Pryadko [269] on the existence of a
threshold for such qLDPC codes under independent noise model to local stochastic adversarial
noise model. We go through the proof of this result.
To obtain this threshold, we first consider a decoding algorithm that receives a syndrome string
s(F) ∈Zl
2 (corresponding to generators g1, . . . , gl of stabilizer group Si) from Pauli error F ∈ˆPni
and outputs a correction Pauli operator E ∈ˆPni with lowest weight that is consistent with syndrome
s(F) (choosing one at random if there are multiple minimum-weight consistent Paulis). This decoder
is successful whenever EF ∈Si and fails whenever EF ∈N(Si)\Si, since EF is a logical operation
and has a trivial syndrome as s(EF) = s(E) + s(F) = 0l because s(E) = s(F). When the latter
happens, the decoding procedure results in a logical error. Now we introduce a graph we call the
code adjacency graph that captures how the qubits are connected by the stabilizers.
Definition 14.2: Code adjacency graph
For an n-qubit stabilizer code Q with stabilizer generators g1, . . . , gl, a simple graph G is the
code adjacency graph of Q if its vertices V (G) and edges E(G) are given by
V (G) = [1, n]
and
E(G) = {(u, v) : ∃i ∈[1, l], u, v ∈supp(gi)} .
(14.3)
Namely, the vertices of G are labels for the n qubits and two vertices are connected by an
edge iff the two corresponding qubits are in the support of a stabilizer generator.
For an (r, c)-qLDPC code, each qubit is involved in at most c generators and each generator has
support on at most r qubits. Therefore, the degree of each vertex u in the adjacency graph G of an
(r, c)-qLDPC as defined in Definition 14.2 is bounded as
deg(u) ≤(r −1)c .
(14.4)
Note also that a generator gi corresponds to a clique in G, since qubits involved in gi are connected
to each other.
Now, let us call a subset of vertices W ⊆V a cluster if the induced subgraph G(W) is connected.
For a Pauli P ∈ˆPni, we say that a cluster W ⊆supp(P) is a disjoint cluster of P whenever there are
no qubits in supp(P) that are in the border of W, that is, ∀v ∈supp(P)\W, ∀u ∈W, (u, v) /∈E(G).
A disjoint cluster W of P is minimal if there is no N ⊊W such that N is a disjoint cluster of
P. Let us denote the set of all minimal disjoint cluster of P as MDC(P). Now we will show the
following lemma that connects failure of the decoding scheme above to the structure of errors across
the ni qubits.
Lemma 14.1: Decoder failure
If the decoder fails, then there exists a set of connected qubits W ⊆V (G) of size |W| ≥di
such that at least |W|/2 of qubits in it are faulty.
Proof. Suppose that the decoding fails, that is, it chooses a correction Pauli E such that EF ∈
N(S) \ S. Then, consider a cluster M ∈MDC(EF) and EF|M, the restriction of EF to qubits in
cluster M. Note that EF|M has the following properties:
1. supp(EF|M) = M, since if there is a qubit q ∈M that is not in supp(EF|M) then we can
always find a subset N ⊆M \ {q} that is a disjoint cluster of EF, contradicting that M is
minimal.

(say, g1, . . . , gl′) while EF|V \M corresponds to the remaining l −l′ (say, gl′+1, . . . , gl). This
is because supp(EF|M) is disconnected from supp(EF|V \M), that is, (u, v) /∈E for all u ∈
supp(EF|M) and v ∈supp(EF|V \M) (by the definition of a disjoint cluster). So, s(EF|M) is

3. EF|M ∈N(Si). The only generators with support on M are g1, . . . , gl′, each giving syndrome

4. There exists M ∈MDC(EF) where EF|M /∈Si (it is not a stabilizer). If there is no such M,

Consider W ∈MDC(EF) such that EF|W /∈Si (c.f., property 4 above). By property 3, it

s(F|V \W ) follows from property 2 above that s(EF|V \W ) = 0).
This contradicts that E is a

So,

(14.6)
which implies that wt(F|W ) ≥|W|/2, completing the proof

14.3
Decoding threshold for noisy syndrome extraction
In the last section, we showed that a family of qLDPC codes Jni, ki, diK with growing physical
qubits and distance (ni →∞and di →∞as i →∞) can be decoded with exponential logical error
suppression whenever the physical error rate is below a certain threshold, under local stochastic
noise model. However, this assumes that the syndrome extraction process itself is noiseless, that is,
the syndrome extraction (SE) circuit implementing stabilizer measurements is ideal. In a realistic
scenario, components inside the SE circuit can themselves be faulty. To model a realistic scenario
157


## Page 158


Fault tolerance II
more faithfully, besides qubit errors on the input state, we also need to consider a circuit-level noise
model in the QEC procedure, which consists of both qubit errors and measurement errors in the
SE circuit.
Theorem 4 of [229] shows that even if SE circuits are noisy, a qLDPC code family whose pa-
rameters satisfy the aforementioned properties can also be decoded with exponential logical error
suppression given that initial error rate per qubit ˜p, error rate per qubit at each SE cycle p, and bit-
flip error rate per syndrome bit q are below certain thresholds, under the circuit-level local stochastic
noise model. Such error suppression is obtained when we repeat the SE circuit T = di many times
where syndromes between subsequent SE cycles are compared to infer syndromes reliably. In this
section, we discuss the proof of this result in detail.
14.3.1
Errors in the syndrome extraction circuit
As mentioned above, we first need to consider input errors, syndrome bit errors (resulting from faulty
measurements), and gate errors. For this, we consider the following errors at each step t ∈[1, T] in
the SE cycle:
1. Ft ∈Pn the actual n-qubit physical Pauli errors at timestep t and Et ∈Pn physical Pauli
errors deduced by the decoder (i.e., which physical errors the decoder thinks happened at
round t).

syndrome bit errors deduced by the decoder (i.e., which bit-flip errors the decoder thinks
happened in round t).
3. F1 and E1 are actual and deduced input errors, respectively.
Physical errors in Ft are those that can trigger a detection in round t SE. We can express physical
error Ft and syndrome bit error Bt as Ft = Ft,1 ⊗· · · ⊗Ft,n and Bt = (Bt,1, . . . , Bt,l) where Ft,q
are Pauli errors on qubit q at timestep t and Bt,j are bit-flip errors on syndrome measurement for
stabilizer generator gj at round t. To illustrate this, consider the following example.

(1, 0, 0) = s2 + s3 = ∆3 = σ(F3) + B2 + B3 = (1, 0, 0) ,

which indicates that we observe that the first syndrome bit is flipped.
Syndrome change ∆t provides information to the decoder about any errors between timestep t
and t −1. Up to the point after SE at timestep t is completed, the decoder have deduced errors
E1, E2, . . . , Et consistent with observed syndromes s1, . . . , st, namely
σ(F1) + B1 = s1 = σ(E1) + C1 ,
σ(F2F1) + B2 + B1 = s2 = σ(E2E1) + C2 + C1 ,
...
σ(Ft . . . F1) + Bt + Bt−1 = st = σ(Et . . . E1) + Ct + Ct−1 .
(14.22)
Since σ(Ft . . . F1) = σ(Ft) + σ(Ft−1 . . . F1) and σ(Et . . . E1) = σ(Et) + σ(Et−1 . . . E1), we obtain a
consistency relation between actual errors and deduced errors:
σ(Ft) + Bt + Bt−1 = ∆t = σ(Et) + Ct + Ct−1
, ∀t ≥2
and
σ(F1) + B1 = ∆1 = σ(E1) + C1 .
(14.23)

t=1 EtFt is the overall discrepancy in the

there would be no discrepancy at the end of the SE cycles, that is, QT
t=1 EtFt = QT
t=1 FtFt = I.

14.3.2
Syndrome adjacency graph and error clusters
Now that we have laid the groundwork for syndromes and syndrome errors, before proceeding to
the main body of the proof for the error correction threshold of qLDPC codes with syndrome mea-
surement errors, we need to define one more object to help us with relating qubit errors, syndrome
errors, and the stabilizer checks. Similar to the proof for a threshold for qLDPC codes with no
measurement errors in Section 14.2, we will represent qubit errors and measurement bit-flip errors
using a graph that take into consideration both data qubits and syndrome bits over T SE cycles.
Definition 14.3: Syndrome adjacency graph
For an n-qubit stabilizer code Q with stabilizer generators g1, . . . , gl and a positive integer
T, a simple graph Γ is the syndrome adjacency graph of Q over T SE cycles if its vertices
V (Γ) = X(Γ) ∪B(Γ) and edges E(Γ) = K(Γ) ∪L(Γ) are given by
X(Γ) = {(x, t) : x ∈[1, n], t ∈[1, T + 1]}
,
B(Γ) = {(b, t) : b ∈[1, l], t ∈[1, T]}
,
K(Γ) =

((x, t), (y, t)) ∈X(Γ) × X(Γ) : ∃i ∈[1, l], x, y ∈supp(gi)
	
,
L(Γ) =

((b, t), (x, t′)) ∈B(Γ) × X(Γ) : x ∈supp(gb), t′ ∈{t, t + 1}
	
.
(14.27)
161


## Page 162


Fault tolerance II
Namely, qubit vertices X(Γ) are labels for the n qubits at before and after each T SE cycles,
syndrome vertices B(Γ) are labels of syndrome bits in each of the T SE cycles, edges K(Γ)
connects any two qubits at timestep t involved in a stabilizer generator, and edges L(Γ)
connects syndrome vertices at timestep t to qubit vertices at timestep t and t + 1.
For an (r, c)-qLDPC code family Q1, Q2, . . . with parameters Jn1, ki, diK, each qubit vertex (x, t)
is connected to at most other r −1 qubit vertices at timestep t, connected to at most c syndrome
vertices at timestep t, and to at most c syndrome vertices at timestep t + 1. So the degree of (x, t)
is at most 2c + r −1. On the other hand, each syndrome vertex (b, t) is connected to at most r
qubit vertices at timestep t −1 and at most r qubit vertices at timestep t. Thus, any syndrome
vertex (b, t) has degree of at most 2r. So, a syndrome adjacency graph Γ corresponding to any code
Qi from an (r, c)-qLDPC code family Q1, Q2, . . . must have degree of at most max{2c + r −1, 2r},
which is a constant in ni.
Now consider actual errors F1, . . . , FT and B1, . . . , BT , deduced errors E1, . . . , ET and C1, . . . , CT ,
and final correction G. Then consider the following sets of “marked” vertices:
Xmrk = {(x, t) ∈X(Γ) : t ∈[1, T] , (x, t) ∈supp(EtFt)} ,
Bmrk = {(b, t) ∈B(Γ) : (Bt + Ct)b = 1} ,
XT+1
mrk = {(x, T + 1) ∈X(Γ) : (x, T + 1) ∈supp(G)} .
(14.28)
Namely, we mark qubit vertices (x, t) ∈X(Γ) if qubit x at time t ∈{1, . . . , T} is in the support of
Pauli error EtFt, qubit vertices (x, T +1) at time T +1 that are in the support of final correction Pauli
G, and syndrome bit vertices (b, t) at time t ∈{1, . . . , T} that are in the support of Bt +Ct. For the
rest of the proof, we will look into largest subsets of marked vertices from Vmrk = Xmrk∪Bmrk∪Xt+1
mrk
that induces a connected subgraph which we call a cluster. More precisely, K ⊆Vmrk is a cluster
if (1) for all pairs of vertices u, v ∈K there exist a sequence of edges (u, w1), (w1, w2), . . . , (wl, v)
such that w1, . . . , wl ∈K and (2) for all vertices v ∈K, all marked neighbors of v are K. As an
illustration, consider the following example.

14.3.3
Properties of error clusters
As we discussed above, a cluster is a connected induced graph of the syndrome adjacency graph
Γ consisting of marked nodes determined by qubit errors F = F1, . . . , FT , syndrome errors B =
B1, . . . , BT , and the errors (E = E1, . . . , ET , C = C1, . . . , CT , G) deduced by the decoder through-
out the circuit. Clusters in Γ capture how all errors affect the final residual error P = G QT
t=1 EtFt
at the end of the circuit, which determines whether the decoding is successful. In the following, we
will show properties of clusters in a series of lemmas that we use later to characterize logical errors
and bound logical error rates to obtain thresholds. First, we show that the residual error PK caused
by errors in cluster K must have a trivial syndrome.
Lemma 14.2: Connected cluster trivial syndrome
For a connected cluster K, let PK = G|K
QT
t=1 Et|KFt|K where F is the actual errors, E
the deduced errors, G the final correction, and E|K is the restriction of Pauli E to K. Then
σ(PK) = 0, that is, it has a trivial syndrome.
Proof. Recall that for all bits b ∈{1, . . . , l} we have the consistency condition
σ(EtFt)b = (Bt−1 + Bt + Ct−1 + Ct)b , ∀t ∈[2, T]
and
σ(E1F1)b = (B1 + C1)b ,

and
σ(E1|KF1|K)b + σ(E1|K′F1|K′)b = (B1 + C1)b .
(14.31)
By the definition of a cluster, all marked qubit vertices (x, t) and (x, t+1) in K′ for any qubit x ∈[ni]

The syndrome triviality of PK could mean two things: (1) PK is a non-trivial logical error or
(2) PK is a stabilizer. The following lemma shows that if the residual error P = G QT
t=1 EtFt at the
end of the T cycles is a non-trivial logical operator (thus resulting in a logical error), then there is
a cluster K which contains errors PK which itself is a logical operator and therefore has weight of
at least the distance d of the code.
Lemma 14.3: Error weight lower bound
If P = G QT
t=1 EtFt is a non-trivial logical operator of qLDPC code Q with stabilizer group
S and parameter Jn, k, dK (i.e., P ∈N(S) \ S), then there exists a cluster K such that
wt(PK) ≥d for PK = G|K
QT
t=1 Et|KFt|K.
Proof. Let H = QT
t=1 EtFt so that P = GH and assume that it is a non-trivial logical operator,
that is, GH ∈N(S) \ S. We will then show that PK is in N(S) \ S for at least one cluster K of P,
that is, it is a logical operator.
Consider clusters K1, . . . , Km of P so that P = Qm
i=1 PKi. By Lemma 14.2, for each i ∈[m] we
have σ(PKi) = 0 so that PKi ∈N(S). Now, assume that PKi ∈S for all i ∈[m]. Then this means
that P = Qm
i=1 PKi ∈S, which contradicts the assumption that GH ∈N(S)\S. So, PK ∈N(S)\S
must hold. All non-trivial logical operator of Q must have weight of at least d. So, wt(PK) ≥d.
By how we set our decoder, it chooses minimum weight Paulis that are consistent with the

weight property of deduced errors restricted to any cluster K also holds.

Now we show the last property of clusters in a graph. This is the same property we used in the

Lemma 14.7: Upper bound on number of clusters [229, Lemma 2]
For any graph Γ with maximum degree z′, the number of connected clusters K of size |K| = s
containing vertex v ∈V (Γ) is at most (z′e)s−1 and the total number of connected clusters of
size s is at most |V (Γ)|(z′e)s−1.
Bounding logical error probability
Now we will bound the logical error rate that grows either
as O(nT(p′/pi)d), O(n(p′/pf)d), O(n(p′′/pi)d), or O(n(p′′/pf)d), according to different types of
clusters causing the logical error, for some pi, pf and for p′ = max{p, q} and p′′ = max{˜p, p, q}. First,
let us consider errors and corrections G, E1, B1, . . . , ET , BT , F1, C1, . . . , FT , CT so that a decoding
failure results in a logical error P = G QT
t=1 EtFt ∈N(S) \ S, which restriction to a cluster K
yields PK = G|K

the size |K| of such “bad” cluster must satisfy |K| ≥wt(PK) ≥d. So, the probability of a logical
error can be bounded as
Pr[Logical error] ≤Pr[There is a cluster K s.t. wt(PK) ≥d]

Now, let us consider four types of logical errors according to the bad cluster K causing it:

14.4
Threshold theorem for constant-overhead FTQC scheme
Gottesman’s FT scheme [229] simulates logical quantum circuit C with k qubits and f(k) locations
using O(k) physical qubits at any given time and O(f(k)poly k) circuit locations. Namely, this
scheme has a constant O(k)
k
= O(1) space overhead and polynomial O(f(k)poly k)
f(k)
= O(poly k) time
overhead. For simplicity, we also assume that the circuit C consists of Clifford gates {H, CNOT, S}
and a non-Clifford gate T.
The code family {Qi} with parameters Jni, ki, diK used in the scheme is (r, c)-LDPC: each sta-
bilizer generator acts on at most r qubits and each qubit involved in at most c generators, for
constants r, c independent of i. Moreover, we also demand some additional properties of the code
family:
1. Encoding rate R = limi→∞ki/ni > 0.
2. Distance di →∞.
3. Growth of number of physical qubits: 0 < ni −ni−1 < nβ
i−1 for β > 0.
4. If thresholds ˜p < p0, p < p1, and q < p2 are satisfied and T(ni) SE rounds are performed,
then the code is decodable with logical error rate pL = O(1/g(ni)), for g(n) = Ω(poly n) and
T(n) = poly n.
Other than the qLDPC code family, below we list key ingredients and ideas that go into this FTQC
scheme, each of which we discuss in detail:
1. Block-encode k logical qubits into M = ⌈k
ki ⌉blocks of (r, c)-qLDPC codes Qi satisfying the
conditions above (for an illustration comparing this block encoding scheme to the concatenated
code encoding in Section 13, see Figure 27).
2. Sequential FT circuit: one logical operation at a time.
3. Cycle error correction (EC) across M blocks of Qi, sharing ancillas.
4. Shor-style syndrome extraction (SE) with cat-state ancillas.
5. Knill-style teleportation for logical gates and measurements with offline ancilla preparation
using concatenated codes.
First, though, we discuss the fault tolerance and noise model used for the scheme.
174

Figure 27: Illustration of encoding by concatenation (left) and block encoding (right). In both figures, blue
circles represent physical qubits and green circles represent logical qubits. On the left-hand side, each green
circle represent an outer J4, 1K code, where each one of its four qubits (yellow, rounded squares) is itself a
J4, 1K code (where the four physical qubits are the blue circles inside a yellow rounded square). Thus, the 12
logical qubits use 12×16 = 192 physical qubits through two levels of concatenation. On the right-hand side,
each red, rounded rectangle is a J16, 3K code, where the 16 physical qubits are represented by blue circles
and the 3 logical qubits by green circles. Thus, with 4 copies of the J16, 3K code, we can encode 12 logical
qubits using 4 × 16 = 64 physical qubits.
14.4.1
Basic model and simplified model of fault tolerance
Here we adopt the basic model of fault tolerance. In this model, we consider locations of a circuit C,
denoted by loc(C), as the set of all potentially faulty components, which includes state preparation,
gates, measurements, and wait locations. For example, consider the following circuit with two state
preparation locations, one single-qubit gate location, one two-qubit gate location, one measurement
location, and two wait locations:

We adopt the local stochastic noise model as defined in Definition 14.1 so that for an error rate p,
the probability of faults in a subset of locations S ⊆loc(C) is at most p|S|.
Later, for the fault tolerance analysis of the error correction cycles, we will instead use a simplified
model of fault tolerance. In the simplified model, we instead consider three different types of faults
with local-stochastic noise: initial input errors with rate ˜p, output data qubit errors with rate pD,
and syndrome bit-flip errors with rate q. Note that this is the model that we use in the qLDPC
code decoding threshold in Section 14.3. To illustrate this more concretely and to compare between
the basic model and simplified model of fault tolerance in syndrome extraction circuits, consider
the following T iterations of a generic syndrome extraction circuit in the basic model:

With these FT models and using the ingredients for the FTQC scheme we listed above, we can
show that FTQC with constant space overhead can be achieved whenever error rates are below some
thresholds. In particular, this FTQC scheme uses a family of qLDPC codes {Qi}i with parameters
Jni, ki, diK satisfying: (1) a non-zero asymptotic rate R = limni→∞ki
ni , (2) a condition on the growth
of number of physical qubits ni, and (3) existence of a decoding with logical error rate and residual
error rate guarantees. Using this qLDPC code family, we can show that a k-qubit logical circuit
C with f(k) locations can be simulated by a circuit ˜C using O(k) qubits at any given time and
O(f(k)poly (k)) total number of physical locations, where the output distribution of ˜C is ϵ-far from
C. This is formalized in the following threshold theorem.
Theorem 14.1: Constant-overhead FTQC threshold [229, Theorem 1]
Let {Qi} be a family of QEC codes with:
1. Qi is an Jni, kiK (r, c)-LDPC code, for constants r, c.
2. As ni →∞, ki
ni →R > 0.
3. 0 < ni −ni−1 < nβ
i−1 for some β > 0.
4. Decoding guarantees local-stochastic noise: Under local stochastic noise (with initial-
ization error rate ˜p per qubit, error rate in QEC cycle pD per data qubit per syndrome
extraction (SE), syndrome bit flip rate q), ˜p < p0, pD < p1, and q < p2 implies logical
error O(1/g(ni)) after decoding and residual errors ≤p0/3 given a polynomial T(ni)
syndrome extractions each with depth h(ni) if there is no logical errors. (T, h, g are
non-decreasing functions.)
Choose α < 1 such that 0 < αβ < 1. Then, for all η > 1, ϵ > 0 and for all polynomials
f(n) = o(g(nα)), there exists pT (η) and k0(η, f, ϵ) such that for all sequential circuit C with
k > k0 qubits and f(k) locations, there exists a simulation ˜C using at most ηk/R physical
qubits. If local-stochastic error rate p satisfies p < pT (η), statistical distance between ˜C and
C is at most ϵ > 0. Furthermore for α′ = max(α, αβ), simulation ˜C uses:

2. Classical computation time of O(h(2kα′) + log log(k/ϵ)) per logical step.
3. If T(ni) polynomial, then | ˜C| = poly k; if decoding can be done in polynomial time,
then classical computation time complexity is also polynomial.
Note that the parameters β > 0 and α < 1 determine the number of locations loc( ˜C) and the
number of classical computation steps. We will later see how these parameters are chosen. Also,
note that this threshold theorem holds for big enough logical circuits C, that is, ones that uses
k > k0 qubits, where k0 also depends on these parameters.
14.4.2
Error correction
We now discuss how error correction is performed in this FTQC protocol, the rate at which errors
remain, and how much overhead it incurs. To perform reliable error correction, we measure each
syndrome T(ni) = poly (ni) times, where each syndrome measurement is done using Shor’s fault-
tolerant error correction (Shor EC) scheme (c.f. Section 13.2.1). For each stabilizer generator g with
weight w ≤r, Shor EC for measuring g consists of three steps:
1. Prepare an ancilla cat state
1
√
2(|0w⟩+ |1w⟩) fault-tolerantly (will be discussed below).
2. Apply controlled-Pauli gates from cat qubits to data qubits.
3. Measure each ancilla qubit in X-basis.

Since each stabilizer generator has weight of at most r, a Shor EC needs an additional r′ = O(r)

a single round of Shor EC will need r′M(ni −ki) ancilla qubits if we measure all stabilizers in all
code-blocks simultaneously. To prevent this blowup, we split the M blocks of Jni, ki, diK code to
s sets, each containing M/s blocks. Then, we measure only the syndromes of M/s code-blocks at
any given time in the QEC cycle while all the other (s −1)M/s blocks wait until we measure all
syndromes of the M/s code-blocks before we move on to the next M/s blocks. This way, we only
need additional r′M(ni −ki)/s ancilla qubits for QEC that we reuse to do Shor EC on different
blocks in cycle. Since we need to prepare an average of

In this FTQC scheme, we use Knill’s gate teleportation scheme proposed in [24], which allows for
an implementation of a universal fault-tolerant gate set [73] by preparing a logical ancilla state

14.4.4
Initial error rate in the simplified FT model
In this FTQC scheme, a QEC cycle consisting of T QEC rounds is performed between subsequent
logical gates. So, faults from the logical gate implementation right before a QEC round are counted
as initial errors for that QEC round with local-stochastic error rate ˜p. In addition to logical gate
faults, the initial errors of a QEC cycle also comes from residual errors from the QEC round right
before the logical gate. As an illustration, consider a logical gate U and two QEC cycles before and

showed in Section 14.3, whenever the decoding is successful.
From our previous discussion on how logical gates are implemented, we can now analyze how
the logical gate faults cause initial errors at the QEC cycle following every logical gate, along with
residual errors from the previous QEC cycle, and determine its local-stochastic error rate ˜p. A
physical data qubit error caused by logical gate faults comes from the following two sources:
1. Ancilla factory: if there are B locations in an ancilla factory, then faults in it cause a physical
data qubit error with probability Bp.
2. In a teleportation circuit, a data qubit is involved in a Hadamard, a CNOT, a measurement,
and a logical Pauli correction. Thus faults on these locations will cause a physical data qubit
error with probability 4p.
Note that a logical Clifford U may be a two-qubit gate across two code blocks, such as a CNOT,
which will propagate residual errors from the previous QEC cycle across code blocks. So the physical

14.4.6
Constant space overhead
We now show that this FTQC scheme incurs a constant space overhead. In particular, for a logical
circuit C using k qubits, we can construct a circuit ˜C simulating C using O(1) qubits at any given
time. Note that the total number of physical qubits used in ˜C is given by
Total physical qubits = Ndata + NQEC + Nop ,
(14.114)
where Ndata is the number of physical data qubits, NQEC is the total number of physical qubits
used for ancillas in QEC at any given time, and Nop is the total number of physical qubits used
for ancillas in logical operations (logical gates, measurements, and state preparation) at any given
time.

# classical steps per logical step = O(h(2kα′) + log log(k/ε)) .
(14.136)
With this, we conclude the proof of Theorem 14.1.
14.4.8
Further improvements
One shortcoming of Gottesman’s constant-overhead FTQC result is that it makes no promise
whether decoding can be done in polynomial time (in general it is indeed a hard problem). This
was solved first in the work by Fawzi et al. [273], where the general Gottesman FTQC scheme is
instantiated using quantum expander code
(which is a hypergraph product code
[232] with
classical expander codes as seed codes). The quantum expander code family has a parameter of
JΘ(ki), ki, Θ(√ki)K and enables a single-shot decoder with O(log(ki)) runtime (even with noisy
syndrome measurements).
More recently, a work by Nguyen and Pattison [271] uses quantum locally-testable codes (LTC)
which allows for single-shot decoding that incurs only a constant-time classical computation per
logical quantum timestep. Moreover, they also showed that an improvement from O(polylog(k))
overhead in concatenated ancilla factory to (log k)o(1) by using optimized stabilizer state and magic
state distillation.
14.5
Magic state distillation
Magic state distillation is one of the most, if not the most, widely used subroutines in FTQC schemes
to enable a universal fault-tolerant computation based on stabilizer codes. In general, a magic state
distillation protocol starts with multiple copies of noisy magic states ρ⊗m followed by an encoding
to logical magic states ρ⊗k in some quantum error-correcting code (for k < m), where errors can be
detected or corrected to produce less noisy magic states ˜ρ⊗k. Then, one can implement non-Clifford
gates using gate teleportation on ˜ρ⊗k. Thus, while the Eastin-Knill theorem obstructs a transversal
implementation of a universal transversal logical gate set on any code (see Section 13.9), one could
still perform universal quantum computation using a stabilizer code with transversal logical Clifford
gates and magic state distillation. Here, we discuss a magic state distillation protocol proposed
in [274] using the J4, 2, 2K
error-detecting stabilizer code.

14.5.3
Distilling two magic states using error-detection
Two logical magic states |H⟩encoded in the J4, 2, 2K code can be obtained probabilistically using
10 resource states ρH as follows:
1. Encode two resource states ρH to a J4, 2, 2K code block.
2. Perform the logical H1H2 measurement on the code block. Here, we use a J4, 2, 2K-code gadget
that implements the circuit in Eq. (14.142) by decomposing the control Hadamard gate as
in Eq. (14.143) and the π/4 Y -rotation gadget as in Eq. (14.144). This gadget consumes eight
resource states ρH, which will be described shortly.
3. A −1 outcome from measuring H ⊗H indicates that one copy of the encoded ρH state has
a Y error and a +1 outcome indicates that either both encoded resource states are |H⟩or
| −H⟩.
4. Run syndrome extraction of the code by measuring the two stabilizer generators s1, s2 in Eq. (14.137)
to detect single-qubit errors, then unencode the J4, 2, 2K code block.
5. Accept the two output resource states if: (1) H1H2 measurement outcome is +1 and (2) there
is no −1 syndrome. If at least one of the conditions is not satisfied, discard the output state
and start over.
